"Rowan calls nothing." Perrin did not look at Kiva. "Rowan's on her Ember all day, and she's got Dray on her, because Dray's going to go wherever Rowan goes, and that's two of Twenty-Eight's four doing nothing but each other. Rowan, you watch Dray's feet and your own, and you keep your mouth shut about everybody else's."

Kiva looked at the ring on the slate round Pru.

It was the thing she had learned to do in a van at a hem, and the thing she had never once done on a lane floor in her life. On every floor she had stood on since she was thirteen she had been the one who said *now* and *mine* and *yours* and *set.* She had said them because she had been inside the gaps and knew when they came. It had never occurred to her that the call might be somebody else's place, and not hers.

"Finch's," she said.

Pru had gone scarlet. She did not look away.

"I'll call it as I do," said Pru. "Not as you do. I watch hands. I'll watch feet as well, today, because I watched Treen learn to off you. I'll not be good at it." She put her capped hands flat on the table. "And you'll not say a word, even if I'm wrong. Not *far edge.* Not anything. If you say one word I'll stop and listen to you, and that's two places."

"Not a word," said Kiva.

"Promise."

"I promise."

Perrin looked at the two of them, catcher and catcher, across his slate. Something went across his face and did not stay.

"Twenty-Eight," he said. "Marten's their captain and their catcher, and he's slow, and he's honest, and he calls off the litter. Their Hold's good. Their still door's big and planted and doesn't like to move. And Dray." He tapped the slate. "Dray on her feet. Turns badly to her left. She knows it. She'll stand with her left to the hurdle so she's always turning right."

"She'll have thought of it," said Kiva.

"She thinks of everything." Perrin put the chalk down. "That's the other thing. She's watched every lane we've stood since the gate. She knows the elbows. She knows the hen. She knows Pryce walks her wall from the far end." He looked round at them. "She doesn't know Finch calls the pen. Nobody does. We decided it at this table four minutes ago."

---

The Graving was full at ten, and half of the full had come to look at a girl who dreamed.

Kiva felt them as she came out of the side door onto the floor. It was not like Friday. On Friday the whole town had stood up for Carrow, and Kiva had been one of eight people on a floor. Today it did not stand. It sat, tier after tier, in green and in grey and in the colours of six circuits, and every face in the lower tiers turned as she came out of the door and followed her across the stone to Seven's hurdle, and she felt it on the side of her face and the back of her neck like the sun.

It was not unkind. Somebody in the third tier called *elbows!* as she passed, and somebody near him laughed. But under it there was the other thing, the thing Perrin had said in the shop. They were looking at her as people look at a thing that has been sent for.

She kept her eyes on the stone.

Anwen was at her back. She did not say anything. She walked a pace behind Kiva's left shoulder all the way from the door to the hurdle, as a wall walks behind somebody, and when they got to the hurdle she stood there.

Twenty-Eight came out of the far door. Marten first, slow and square, with the litter's rules in his face. The Hold after him, a tall fair girl Kiva knew by sight from the Low Lane. The still door, very big. And Dray last, on two feet, without a stick, with her face like a shut door and her edge dark on her right fist, walking with her weight a little more on the right than the left.

The Graving stood up for Dray.

It did not stand all at once. It stood in pieces, from the lower tiers upward, the way a field of birds gets up. The green did not know Dray. They knew she had been in a chair in the ninth month, and that she had walked onto the timber pond on two sticks a fortnight ago and onto the Long Quay on one, and that she was walking now on none. They stood for that.

Dray did not look at them. She looked at Kiva across the pen, once, and then at the Commodore lying in the middle with his tarred hat over one eye.

"*Declarations,*" said the marshal.

"*Twenty-Eight,*" said Marten. "*Marten, ward, catcher. Sedge, ward. Bannock, stance. Dray—*" He stopped. He did not say *edge.* Twenty-Eight had nobody with more than one door, and Dray had said in the autumn, in a chair, that she would always declare her own.

"*Dray,*" said Dray herself, before anybody could stop her, "*edge. Declared. Single. Read.*"

At the mast on the north side of the pen, Noa looked up from his desk. So did Polglase. Dray was not under the rule; she had one door. She had asked to be read anyway. The two readers looked at each other, and then Noa threaded a second pen, and wrote.

"*Team Seven,*" said Perrin. "*Pryce, ward. Talley, edge. Finch, ward, catcher. Rowan, Ember, declared, single, by her captain's voice, and no change. Cade, captain, standing out on his healer's card.*"

*And no change.* Kiva heard it go up the stone walls, and heard the Graving hear it, and heard it understand, in pieces, as it had understood Carrow's *single.* There was a sound like a long breath let out, from the Meridian knot in the middle tiers and then from everybody.

The marshal looked at Perrin over his box.

"*No change,*" he said. "That's declared?"

"It's declared."

"Then I'll hold you to it." He turned his glass. "*Ready.*"

The first carry was Twenty-Eight's, and it showed Kiva that Dray had not been idle.

Seven went for the litter on its arms. They had done it twice now, and Kiva went for the head with her Ember lit, glad, under her breastbone, and the low bound took her to the litter a pace ahead of Twenty-Eight's still door. She got her forearms under the head poles, crossed at the wrists. Pru came for the foot.

"*Pryce, south. Talley, north, his hands,*" said Pru.

Anwen's wall came up south. Jude went north, where Twenty-Eight's still door was coming for the head with his big hands open.

Dray did not come for the litter at all.

She walked down the middle of the pen, past the chalk squares, with her edge dark, and stopped two paces from Jude. She stood with her left side toward Seven's hurdle and her weight on her right foot, and she watched Jude's left arm.

Kiva understood it a moment too late. Jude was fighting one-handed, with his edge on his right fist and his left arm held against his chest. He could not guard his left side without turning his whole body. Dray had seen it on Thursday from the tiers, and on Friday against Carrow. She stood two paces off his left side and waited, as a person waits at a door that they know will open.

"*Talley, left,*" said Pru.

Jude turned. He had to turn his whole body to bring his edge round to his left, and while he turned, Twenty-Eight's still door put both hands on the head poles beside Kiva's crossed forearms and simply stood there, planted, heavier than she was. And Dray lit her edge and stepped in on Jude's right, where he had just come from, and touched him on the shoulder.

"*Down,*" said the marshal. "Seven."

Jude knelt.

Kiva felt the head of the Commodore go. It did not go fast. Twenty-Eight's still door leaned from the floor, as Lyle had leaned, as everybody on the coast seemed to have learned in a week, and the head poles came out of the crooks of her elbows a finger at a time, because her forearms were not hands and could not close. Pru had the foot on her arms. Twenty-Eight's Hold put her wall across the litter's middle, between Pru and the head, and leaned it on nobody, and held it there. Marten took the foot poles from under Pru's crossed forearms with his own two good hands, gently, as a man takes a tray from a child.

They walked it home. At the hurdle Dray shut and went over and turned and took the head from under, and the Hold took the foot.

"*Three. And a down. Four to Twenty-Eight.*"

The Graving made the warm sound. It was for Dray. Kiva heard that it was for Dray, and found that she did not grudge it.

"She's on Talley's arm," said Kiva, at the hurdle.

Pru put her hand up. Kiva shut her mouth.

"*I* say who she's on," said Pru. "You promised." She was white, not scarlet. "She's on Talley's left. She's on it because he's got no edge there and she knows he's got to turn. And she's not on you." Pru looked at Kiva. "She's not been near you. She's waiting. She's leaving you be until it's you and her and nobody else."

Kiva had seen that too. She had not said it.

"So Talley doesn't turn," said Pru. "Talley, if she's on your left, you don't turn. You stand. You let her come. If she wants to touch you she's to come round to your right to do it, and she turns badly to her left, so she'll go round the long way, and that's a count and a half she's not near the litter." She looked at Anwen. "Pryce. Their still door's leaning from the floor. Like Lyle."

"I know. Everybody's had it off me this week."

"Then walk round him. Like you walked round Lyle. He's planted. He can lean but he can't follow." Pru looked at the Commodore lying in the middle of the pen. "And we don't carry on our arms. Not this carry. They've had the arms. We carry on Pryce's hands and Talley's hand, the head and the foot, and Rowan and me take it over at the hurdle on our arms, where they can't get at it."

"That's my plan," said Perrin, from outside the pen. "That's Thursday's plan."

"It's Thursday's plan and Friday's arms." Pru did not look round at him. "Captain."

"Go on, then," said Perrin. "It's yours."

The second carry was Seven's, and it was Pru's.

Kiva had stood in a great many lanes. She had never stood in one without calling it, and for the first count after the bell she did not know what to do with her mouth. She kept it shut. She went for the head on her Ember, and the low bound took her there, and she did not put her arms under the poles. She stood at the head with her Ember lit and her hands empty and watched Dray.

Dray watched her.

They stood three paces apart in the middle of the Graving with the litter between them, and neither of them moved, and the lane went on round them as though they were two posts.

"*Pryce, round him. Far end.*"

Anwen's wall came up against Twenty-Eight's still door, who had come for the head and planted. He leaned on it from the floor, and it bowed, a finger. Anwen did not lean back. She walked the far end of her wall round him in a quarter circle, as she had walked it round Lyle on Friday, and the near end came after it, and the big boy was leaning on air with his feet in the stone and nothing in front of him. He had to shut to keep his feet. Jude was there.

"*Down.* Twenty-Eight."

"*Talley, head. Pryce, foot. Walk. North.*"

Anwen and Jude had the Commodore. Anwen had the head in her two good hands; Jude had the foot in his one. They walked it along the north side, close against the posts, and Twenty-Eight's Hold came to stop them and Pru called *hands, south,* and Anwen's wall came up between, low and silver, and went down again.

Dray moved.

She did not come for Kiva. She went for Jude's left side again, fast, with her weight on her right foot and her edge lit, and Jude did not turn. He stood with the foot poles in his right hand and his left arm against his chest and let her come. Dray had to go round to his right to reach him. She went round the long way, as Pru had said, turning to her own right, because her left would not take the turn, and it was a count and a half, and in a count and a half Jude had walked the foot three paces.

At the hurdle Kiva and Pru were on the far side. Kiva did not remember going there. She remembered Pru saying *Rowan, hurdle,* and her Ember's bound, and the hazel going under her, and turning. They put up their forearms crossed at the wrists, both of them, and the Commodore came over the hazel into the crooks of their arms.

"*Over. Three to Seven. A down. Four to four.*"

The Graving did not make the warm sound. It made the other sound, the one it had made on Friday when somebody shouted *elbows.* Somebody shouted it again, and then somebody else shouted *Finch!*, which nobody had ever shouted in the Graving before, and Pru went scarlet from her collar to her hair and did not look up from the Commodore on her arms.

Kiva looked at her across the canvas.

She had not said a word. Not one. She had stood on a lane floor for four minutes with her mouth shut and her Ember lit and watched Selka Dray, and the litter had gone over the hurdle on somebody else's call.

It was the strangest thing she had ever felt on a floor, and it was not unpleasant. It was like being a door in somebody else's house.

The third carry was the glass.

Twenty-Eight had learned. Kiva watched Marten learn it in the pause, at the far hurdle, with Dray beside him, the two of them bent close, Marten slow and Dray quick. When the bell went, Twenty-Eight's still door did not go for the head. He went to Seven's end of the pen and planted three paces short of Seven's hurdle, square, in the way of anything that might come home.

Twenty-Eight's Hold put her wall across the north side, along the posts, where Seven had walked twice.

And Dray went for the litter.

Kiva's Ember took her there in one bound. She got to the head at the same moment as Dray got to the middle, and they stopped, two paces apart, with the Commodore lying between them on the stone and his tarred hat over one eye. The glass was turning. Nobody else was near. Marten was at the foot, slow, coming; Pru was calling Anwen and Jude somewhere behind; the still door was planted at Seven's end; the Hold's wall was along the north. It was Kiva and Dray and the litter, in the middle of the pen, the way Dray had wanted it since the ninth month.

The litter's head lay a pace inside Twenty-Eight's half. The middle was on the chalk.

Kiva could see what a crossing would do. She could see it as plainly as she had ever seen anything. Her hand door, the ward, low and flat at her hip, a wall between her and Dray's edge; then the count, *one, two, three,* and the gap, a quarter of a count with nothing open; and then her Ember, glad, and the low bound back with the head on her forearms, two paces, three, out of Dray's reach and across the chalk into Seven's half before Dray could come round to her left. It was her own certified crossing, hand to Ember. She had made it six times on two masts. She could feel exactly how it would go.

And Dray would be waiting on the third count. Dray had been waiting on it since a chair in the ninth month. Dray would hear Perrin say *Rowan, changing on three,* and she would know to the quarter count when the ward came down, and she would bring her edge into the gap.

Kiva did not know whether Dray would be fast enough. That was the thing. She had been inside the gap. She knew how short it was. She thought, very likely, that she would be out of it before Dray's edge came down, and the litter would be in Seven's half, and the lane would be Seven's on the glass by two clear points.

Outside the pen, at the hurdle, Perrin had opened his mouth.

He had not said anything. He was standing with his hands on the hazel and his mouth open on the first word of a count, looking at her across twenty paces of stone, and she knew what the word was. He was the captain. It was his voice that declared her. It was his to say, if she let him.

She shook her head. Once, very slightly.

Perrin shut his mouth.

Dray had seen it. Kiva saw her see it. Dray had been standing two paces off with her weight on her right foot and her edge lit and her sunk grey eyes not on Kiva's face but on Perrin's mouth, waiting for the count. She saw Perrin's mouth shut. She saw Kiva's head move. And she understood, and her face did something that Kiva had never seen it do. It did not open. But for an instant it was not a door. It was a person who has been told a thing she knew already, and finds that she minds it after all.

Then she came in.

Kiva bounded. She had one door and she used it. She got her forearms under the head poles and went back on her Ember's low bound with the Commodore's head on her arms, dragging, the weight of the whole canvas dummy pulling her down as the sleeve had pulled her down over the channel at Mallow, and she got a pace and a half. The head came across the chalk. The middle came after it.

Dray did not go round to her left. Kiva had thought she might, and had bounded to Dray's left on purpose, toward the turn she did not like. Dray did not take the turn. She stepped across instead, with her right foot, the long way, and it was a count and a half and Kiva was a pace and a half off with a hundredweight on her arms and nothing open but the Ember, and that was not enough. Dray's edge came down flat on her back between the shoulder blades, quite lightly, as lightly as a woman puts her hand on a child's head.

"*Down,*" said the marshal. "Seven."

Kiva let go. She had to; a downed fighter lets go. The head of the Commodore went down on the stone a foot inside Seven's half. She knelt beside it and did not look at Dray.

She counted ten.

She had lost the exchange. She had known she would, since a quarter past seven, at three feet, across two beds. She had chosen to. It did not feel like a thing that had been done to her. It felt like the other thing she had done this week: a thing set down where anybody could see it, Dray and the tiers and Perrin at the hurdle with his mouth shut, and that was all.

"*Hands, Marten, foot,*" said Pru.

Kiva heard it from her knees. She did not look up. She heard Marten's slow square steps come round the foot of the litter to drag it back east across the chalk, and Jude's step after him, and the marshal.

"*Down.* Twenty-Eight."

"*Pryce, over it,*" said Pru.

Anwen's wall came up across the litter's middle. She did not lean it on anybody. She set it there, low and silver, a pace long, over the Commodore's canvas belly like a lid on a box, and stood behind it with her heels right. Twenty-Eight's Hold came to take the foot, and the wall was in the way of her hands, and she could not lift what she could not reach. Dray came round to the head, where Kiva was kneeling, and could not touch the litter while Kiva knelt beside it, because nobody touches a downed fighter, and Kiva was kneeling with her shoulder against the head poles, not holding them, not touching them, only there.

Kiva had not thought of that. She had not planned it. She had knelt where she was put.

Dray stood over her with her edge lit and looked down at her, and Kiva looked up at Dray, and neither of them said anything.

The glass ran out.

"*Time,*" said the marshal. He came down off his box and looked, and walked round the Commodore twice. "*In Seven's half. By a foot.*" He went back up. "*A point to Seven. A down each way.*" He wrote. "*Six to Seven. Five to Twenty-Eight.*"

The Graving did not make the warm sound, or the other sound. It made a sound that Kiva had never heard a crowd make. It was the sound of a great many people who have watched a thing they did not understand, and know that they did not understand it, and are talking to each other about what it was.

Kiva got up off her knees.

Dray was still standing over the head of the Commodore. Her edge was dark now. She stood with her weight on her right foot and her face shut, looking at Kiva, and for a moment Kiva thought she was going to walk away without a word, as she walked away from everything.

"You'd have had it," said Dray. "With the count. Hand to Ember. I was waiting on three."

"I know you were."

"I'd have had you in the gap."

"Maybe," said Kiva. "Maybe not. I've been in it. It's short."

Dray was silent. Her jaw moved, once.

"That's a thing I'll not know now," she said. "Ever. Not on this coast. Not next year. You'll be on one in fourteen by then, if your healer's right, and you'll not spend it on a lane." Her voice was quite flat. "You chose that. At three feet, this morning. And on the floor, with your head."

"Yes."

"I'll not thank you for it."

"I didn't ask you to."

"No." Dray looked down at the Commodore, at the foot of canvas lying across the chalk on Seven's side. "You knelt by the head. Nobody touches a downed fighter. I couldn't reach it past you."

"I didn't mean to. I knelt where I was."

"I know you didn't. That's what makes it worse." Dray turned to go. Then she turned back, as Carrow always did, and as Dray never had. "It was declared," she said. "All of it. Before the bell, and at the box. *No change.* I heard your captain say it." She paused. "That's the fairest lane I've ever lost."

She went away up the pen on two feet, with her weight on the right.

Pru was standing at the litter's foot with her capped hands held up and her face scarlet.

"Six," she said. "Six to five."

"You called it," said Kiva.

"I called it. You didn't say a word." Pru looked at her. "Not even when she was on Talley's arm and I didn't see it for a count. I saw you see it. You shut your mouth so hard I heard your teeth."

"I promised."

"I know you did." Pru put her hands down. "That's the first lane I've ever called that nobody called over me." She did not go any redder; she could not. "It's not a favour. You should know that. I'm not grateful. I'm only good."

"I know you are," said Kiva. "I watched."

---

Rhea Sorn sat on the fourth tier above the floor with her hands in her pockets and her slate on her knee, and watched the girl kneel by the litter's head and not look up, and did not write anything on the slate at all.

Pike was beside her. His hands lay on his knees, closed halfway, as they had lain for a fortnight, and would very likely lie now for the rest of his life. He had not said a word through three carries. He said one now.

"Cade," he said.

"I saw."

"He'd his mouth open on the count. She shook her head." Pike's voice was low and dry. "He shut it. That's a captain. I'd have said the count, at his age. I'd have thought it was mine to say." He nearly smiled. "She called nothing all lane. Not a word. Finch had it."

"I saw."

"Did you know she would?"

"No," said Rhea. "I'd have bet against it. I'd have bet my van."

She took her hands out of her pockets and looked at them. They were a Torch's hands, square and scarred across the knuckles from twenty years of cutting from behind a skin, and they had written a great many cards. They had written one in the autumn that said *catcher's second, to Pike,* with *either of us may say no* at the foot of it, and *no seat is promised,* and a date. The date was the 30th. That was eleven days off, and one more night on her van.

She allowed herself one piece of advice a season to anybody she trained, and no more. She had spent the winter's on the girl in the first month, and the spring's on a mud hard at Gannet Creek at one in the morning. She had held to that rule with every corner-hand and second she had ever had. It was the rule that kept them from being hers.

She had kept to the letter of it. She had broken the rest. She had broken it at the Company yard on Wednesday, telling Yeo to ask the girl for her columns before she slept, and calling it *a thing I know about her.* She had been knowing things about the girl since the first month and saying them aloud to people who would act on them. She had told herself it was only a second's due.

It was not. She knew what it was. It was the thing she had burned a card in the autumn to keep from writing. *Mine.*

"You're not writing," said Pike.

"There's nothing to write. It's not my lane."

"You've written every lane she's stood since the gate. On the back of the slate, small, where you think I can't see." He did not look at her. "*Watches feet.* *Called the far edge from sixty paces.* *Kept the week's crossing.* I can read, Rhea. I've been reading your slate since you first ruled it."

Rhea did not answer that.

On the floor, Selka Dray had turned back to say something to the girl over the head of the litter, and the girl was answering, and the Graving was talking to itself in a long low rumble about what it had seen. Rhea watched the girl's face. It was not a face that had lost anything. It was the face of somebody who had put a thing down on a table where anybody could see it, and was standing back from it.

"Twice in a week," said Rhea. "She's done that twice. In a hall last night, and on a floor this morning. Put a thing down and stood back from it." She took up the slate. "I've never in my life done that once."

She did not write on the back of it. She turned it over to the front, where the six columns were, and below them, in the clear space at the foot where she wrote a card before she copied it out fair, she began to write a card.

It was not the girl's card. It was not time for the girl's card, and it was not hers to write until the girl had been asked, and the girl had answered, and either of them had said no. That was the 30th. She would not write it a day before.

She wrote the other one.

*Graywater. Pryce, A., Hold, Meridian, Team Seven. To Doran Mace's ward line on the night van, in her own place and not a relief's, from the first of the fourth month, at a ward-hand's wage. Mace asked for her by name in the autumn and left her name where it was. A card is not a seat. R. Sorn.*

She read it over. She thought of the Pryce girl on the Ropewalk's boards on Thursday morning, teaching a laid Hold with no ward left how to lean from the floor, knowing it would be used against her own four, and doing it anyway. Mace had told her that on Thursday night at the van, laughing. *She'll give it away to anybody who asks properly. That's the best thing about her and the worst.*

Rhea copied the card out fair, onto a white card from her pocket, in Graywater's slate blue, and blew on it.

"That's not hers," said Pike, reading upside down.

"It's Pryce's."

"I know whose it is. I said it's not *hers.*" He looked at the floor, where the girl was walking back to Seven's hurdle with her bound hand against her coat and the Finch girl beside her. "You'll write hers on the 30th."

"I'll ask her on the 30th. If she says no, I'll not write it."

"And if she says yes?"

Rhea put the card in her pocket.

"Then I'll find out what I write," she said. "I don't know yet. That's the truth, and it's the first time I've said a thing like that to you." She stood up, with her slate under her arm. "I'll pin this at the baize. Come on. You can watch me do a thing I've never done, which is give somebody's Hold away to somebody else's line without asking her captain first."

"Cade'll mind."

"Cade'll mind for an hour and then he'll say he'd like it known that he's glad," said Rhea. "I've watched him all week as well."

She went down the tiers toward the floor's edge, a step at a time, and Pike came after her with his half-closed hands held a little away from his sides, as a man carries a thing he has been asked to carry and does not mean to drop.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-38.md

# Chapter 38 — Kiva: The Measure Is Read

Halden License lost to Holloway's second four at one o'clock on Saturday by five points to two, and Kiva watched every carry of it from the sixth tier with her right hand in fresh linen and her knees still stiff from the stone.

She had not meant to watch it so closely. She had come up the tiers after Seven's lane to sit with Aske and eat something, and Aske had put a pasty in her good hand and said *eat,* and she had eaten it. Then Halden had come out of the side door in their black and gold, and she had stopped eating.

Kellan was on the floor. She had half thought he would not be. She had lain awake for an hour last night wondering whether Pettit would stand him down after the Salt Hall. But Pettit had declared him at the box in his heavy flat voice, *Renn, edge,* the same as every other day, and Kellan had gone to his place on the north side of the pen and stood there with his edge dark on his fist and his face giving nothing away.

He fought well. Kiva could see that from the sixth tier. He took Holloway's carry with a touch in the first, as clean as anything she had seen all week, and he read their Hold's feet in the second and was there before she moved. It did not matter. Holloway's second four had been ninth on the Bench and fourth on the Hunt, and nobody in the Graving had watched them all week, and they had watched everybody. They had watched Seven's elbows on Thursday and Friday, and Carrow's word, and the hen, and Lyle's lean. In the third carry their catcher sat on the litter's head with both forearms along the canvas, and their Hold leaned on Halden's wall from the floor, and Holloway's blade stood off Kellan's left side and waited for a count he could not see coming.

"*Five to Holloway,*" said the marshal at the glass. "*Two to Halden.*"

The Graving made the long sound of breath let out. It went round the tiers twice. Everybody in it could add.

"That's the Measure," said Aske, beside her, quite quietly. "Isn't it."

"It's the Ropewalk's," said Kiva, "if they beat Holloway's first four at three."

"And if they don't?"

"Then it's still not Halden's." She had done the sum on the tiers, with her left hand on her knee, as Kellan would have done it. "Halden's sixty-five and the Lane. They'd have needed to win today and stand fourth or better. They've lost a lane. They'll be ninth or tenth in the Lane, on points. Eighty, or eighty-one." She looked down at the floor, where Kellan was walking back to Halden's hurdle with his edge dark and Pettit's hand on his shoulder. "The Ropewalk's fifty-eight. If they win at three, they're first in the Lane, or near it. Eighty-two."

"You'd make a grader," said Aske.

"His mother's a grader," said Kiva.

Below them, at Halden's hurdle, Kellan had stopped. He stood with Pettit's hand still on his shoulder, and he looked up at the tiers, at the Meridian knot, and found Seven in it, and found her. He did not wave. He did a thing Kiva had seen him do once before, at a gate, with a pencil: he held up one finger, and then a second, and then let his hand fall.

Two. By two marks. He had done the sum on the floor, before the glass had stopped running.

---

Old Four lost to Westerley's first four at two, five to two, and Lowen Brask walked off the floor afterward with his notebook open and wrote in it all the way to the side door, as he always did, without looking up.

At three, the Ropewalk's first four came out of the side door for the last lane of the National Measure, and the Graving stood up for Carrow as it had stood up on Friday.

Kiva stood with it. She found that she wanted to.

Holloway's first four had been third on the board after three, and they were good: a big quiet four in dark red who had found in two minutes on the Bench what Seven had needed four to find. They did not lay. They sequenced, as Meridian did, with a still door who planted on his captain's word and a blade who did not move until she was sure. Kiva had watched them beat the Ropewalk's second four on Friday and thought they were the best four in the Measure that nobody talked about.

Carrow declared at the box. Her voice went up the stone walls, low and flat and pleasant.

"*Carrow, edge. Declared. Single.*" She paused. Then, quite deliberately, she added a thing Kiva had never heard a Saltmere captain say. "*And no change.*"

The Graving made a sound. It was very small. Down at the mast on the north side of the pen, Noa looked up from his desk, and then he looked along the tiers until he found Seven, and Kiva saw him find her. Then he looked down and wrote.

"She's had that off Cade," said Aske. "This morning."

"Off Perrin," said Kiva. "Yes."

She had heard it from the Meridian knot in the middle tiers, where Perrin was sitting with his hand pressed to his side. She heard him make a sound that was not quite a word.

The lane went to the Ropewalk, five to three, and it was not close. Kiva watched every carry. Carrow walked on Treen's word, *set,* as she had walked on Friday, and Treen read feet and hands both now, as she had learned on Friday to read them. In the second carry the Ropewalk carried the Commodore down the pen on Tolver's forearms and Treen's, crossed at the wrists, with Lyle leaning from the floor on Holloway's wall. The Graving laughed out loud. It was the warmest sound Kiva had heard it make all week, and some of it was for the Ropewalk and some of it, she thought, was for Pru.

In the third carry, with the Measure on the glass, Holloway's blade came for Carrow at the litter's head, and Treen said *far edge,* two words, once. Carrow turned to her right as if she had been called by name, and Holloway's blade was there, coming round on the side Carrow had not been watching, and Carrow's edge came round and touched her.

"*Down.* Holloway."

"She's had that off you," said Aske.

"Off Pike," said Kiva. "Through me. Through Ivey, I expect, and the Company's yard, and the Marshal's page." She watched Carrow walk back to the Ropewalk's hurdle with one door and nothing open. "It's not anybody's now. It's on a page in every catcher's pocket on the coast."

"*Five to the Ropewalk,*" said the marshal at the glass. "*Three to Holloway.*"

The Graving did not stand. It was already standing. It shouted, all of it, for the first time all week. It shouted for a long time, and Carrow stood in the middle of the pen with her edge dark and her hands at her sides and did not look up at any of it. She looked at Lyle, who was standing by the Ropewalk's hurdle with nothing at her hip. Then she walked across to her and put her hand on her shoulder, and stood there.

---

Mara had asked Kiva at noon to come to the Long Quay at five, and Kiva came. She came alone, because Mara had asked for her alone, and because she found she did not want anybody else.

The coast boat came in under the sea gate at ten to five, out of a grey sea with the light going yellow behind it, and Kiva saw her before the boat was alongside.

She was standing at the bow, by herself, with her hands folded at her waist. She was in plain grey, with a grey cap, tall and thin and very upright, and she was not looking at the quay or the town or the crowd of green that had gathered along the Long Quay's edge because the Mercury had said in the morning that she was coming. She was looking back over her shoulder, past the boat's wake, past the Mole and the arch of the sea gate with its black iron hanging in it. She was looking at the sea.

Kiva felt her doors lie down.

She was forty paces off, on the quay's edge by the third bollard, with Mara beside her and Peverell and Yeo and the sister from the Order a little way along. Forty paces. On the stair of the Order's house, a month ago, she had felt nothing from the doorway, and had felt her doors lie down only in the small room with the fire. Now they lay down at forty paces, across a strip of brown water, all three of them, the Ember under her breastbone and the hand door under her palms and the still door under everything. They did not shut. They lay down where they were, as dogs lie down when somebody comes into a room whom they do not need to watch.

She put her left hand on the bollard. The first two fingers lay on the iron, lightly.

*It keeps time.*

It did, as it always did on this quay, four and four, up through the iron from the granite. Nothing under her fingers had changed. Only her own doors had lain down, and at forty paces, which they had not done at the Order's gate.

"You've felt her," said Mara, very low. Her own left hand was out of her pocket, flat against her side. "So have I, in my hand. From here." She did not take her eyes off the boat. "In her own house she'd nothing to hold against. Here she has the whole town."

The boat came alongside. The gangway went down. The Master of the Order of the Even Hand did not come down it at once. She stood at the bow a little longer, looking at the sea, and the crowd on the quay waited and did not push, because she was not looking at them.

Then she turned and came down the gangway, slowly, a step at a time, with her hands at her waist.

At the foot of it, on the granite of the Long Quay, she stopped. She stood quite still. Kiva saw her feel the town. She saw it go through the long pale calm face like a wince that is not allowed to become one, and stay there for a moment, and go.

Behind her, on the boat's deck, two sisters in grey were handing a cart down off the boat by its shafts, with great care. It was a low flat cart with iron wheels. On its bed, packed in straw, under a grey cloth, there were jars. Kiva could see the shapes of them through the cloth, small and round, in rows. She could see the grey seals on their necks where the cloth had slipped, each with a mark on it like two hands laid flat side by side.

Thrale looked along the quay. Her grey eyes went over the crowd and the white lamps and the green doors and the bills on them, *THE REGIMENT, NOT A STROKE DROPPED,* and came to rest on Mara, and then on Kiva, forty paces off at the bollard. They rested there without searching, as a hand rests on a table.

She did not come nearer.

"Mrs. Rowan," she said. She did not raise her voice. It came along the quay as though there were nothing in between. "Miss Rowan. I'll not come closer. The last time you were in a room with me, you took a fever on my ferry. The ferryman told the sister at the gate that you were carried off his boat on a box." She looked at Kiva. "You've a board to hear read tonight. I'd not have you hot for it."

"Thank you," said Kiva.

"I've seen the sea," said Clemency Thrale. "I said I'd like to, before I was very much older. I've stood at that boat's bow for a day and a half and looked at it." She was quiet a moment. "It doesn't keep time. Nothing in it keeps time. It's every count at once. I'd forgotten." Her folded hands did not move. "And this town keeps one count, in everything, and I can feel it from my feet up, and it's the loudest place I've ever stood."

"There's a hut at the end of the Mole," said Yeo, beside the sister. Her voice was brisk, and a little too careful. "The Company's watch hut. On the sea side, past the gate. It's on the Mole's stone and not in the harbour's water. There's no cell in it. I've had it swept."

"Thank you, officer. I'll lodge there." Thrale looked along the Mole, out toward the sea gate and the arch and the long curved wall going out into the grey. "I'll come into the town for an hour at a time, and no more. An hour in this town is a day's work for me. Tomorrow I'll come for the Board, when it sits, and I'll stand where it puts me, and I'll speak for the cellar." She looked down at the cart, where the two sisters were settling the cloth back over the jars. "I've brought thirty-six. I've not given them. I've brought them. That's a different thing, and the Board will hear the difference tomorrow." She looked at Peverell, whom she had never met, and seemed to know her. "And you'll hear what they cost, keeper. You'll hear it first."

Peverell took her spectacles off their cord.

"I'll hear it," she said. "I've heard a good many prices this fortnight."

"Not this one," said Thrale.

She turned and walked away along the Long Quay toward the Mole, with the two sisters and the cart behind her and the crowd of green opening in front of her without being asked. She walked slowly. Kiva watched her go, and felt her doors get up, one at a time, as she went, and stand, and shake themselves, and the town's count come back up through the iron under her fingers to where it always was.

"She's not a hundred," said Kiva.

"Fifty, by her face," said Mara. "Older than that by her letters." She put her hand back in her pocket. "She left her house without a Master for the first time in eleven years, to bring thirty-six jars to a bank she's never seen, for a town that's never heard of her." She was quiet. "I wrote and asked. I'd not have come, in her place."

"You did come. In the third month. Two hundred miles."

"For a bank I'd been asked to read," said Mara. "Not to give away the only thing my house has."

---

At a quarter to seven Kiva laid the first two fingers of her left hand on the iron rail at the landward lip of the Graving, where she had stood on the first day and looked down eighty feet to a floor with men chalking lines on it.

*It keeps time.*

She wrote it. *Saturday the 19th. A quarter to seven. The rail at the Graving's lip. Yes.*

Then she went down the steps to the floor, because the Board had asked every four that had stood the Measure to stand on the floor of the Graving while it was read.

---

There were ninety-six of them, and the reserves behind, and they stood on the stone at the bottom of the dock in twenty-four rows of four, in the order of the standing after three boards, with Halden License at the front and the twenty-fourth at the back by the floating gate. The tiers went up round them on three sides, full to the iron and glass roof, and the lamps had been lit along every tier, white and steady. At the seaward end, behind the twenty-fourth, the harbour breathed against the gate's black timber.

Seven stood in the ninth row. Perrin had been let stand, by Aske, on condition he did not stand long. He stood at the end of the row with his hand pressed to his side, and Anwen next to him, and Jude, and Kiva at the other end. Pru stood behind them with the reserves, with her capped hands folded in front of her as Thrale folded hers.

The Clerk of the Measure stood on the chalk of the pen's middle line, alone, the smallest thing in the Graving. She did not have a box. She did not have a paper. When she spoke, everybody heard her.

"The National Measure," she said. "At Saltmere. Four boards. Twenty-four fours. No board struck." She waited for the sound that went round the tiers, and it went round and stopped. "The Lane first, since it's the board that's not been read. Then the Measure, from the bottom."

She read the Lane. She read it from the top, because that was how a board was read. *First, the Ropewalk School, first four. Won three. Second, Westerley, first four. Won three. Third, Holloway, second four. Won three.* She read the nine who had won two, in their order, by points for less points against, and Kiva heard Seven in the middle of it, *ninth, Team Seven, Meridian, won two, lost one, sixteen for, twelve against,* and Halden after it, tenth, by two points, and Old Four eleventh by one. She read down to the twenty-fourth, which was a four from the sixth circuit that had lost three and scored four points in three days and stood at the back of the dock by the gate with their heads up.

Then she was quiet for a count of five.

"The Measure," she said. "Marked by place on every board, twenty-four to the first and one to the last, and added. The national license roll takes the first eight. They're licensed for the year to stand a Barrel response under a company officer, whenever the Board warrants one. I'll read from the twenty-fourth."

She read from the twenty-fourth.

It took a long time. She did not hurry it. She read every four's name and its circuit and its marks, and after each one she waited a breath, and the four she had named made whatever sound it made, or none. Kiva stood in the ninth row with her bound hand against her coat and listened to the names go past, twenty-fourth, twentieth, fifteenth, and the Tarn's first four among them, with Trewin standing in the front of his row on frosted feet in boots two sizes too large, and his captain's hat in his hand.

"*Eleventh,*" said the Clerk. "*The Tarn, second four. Fifty-nine.*"

The thin dark girl who was Bridie Hebden's cousin did not move. But her four put their hands on each other's shoulders, all four, in a line.

"*Tenth. The Ropewalk School, second four. Sixty-six.*"

The green in the tiers made the warm sound, not loud.

The Clerk waited. Then she said the next thing in exactly the same voice.

"*Ninth and eighth. There's a tie.*"

The Graving went quiet. It was not the quiet of people waiting. It was the quiet of four thousand people who had all, at the same moment, begun to add.

"*Westerley, second four. Sixty-eight. Team Twenty-Eight, Meridian. Sixty-eight.*" The Clerk did not look at either. "The Board's rule. A tie on marks goes to the four with the best single place on any board. Westerley's second four stood third on the Hunt. Team Twenty-Eight stood first on the Hunt." She paused, for a breath. "*Eighth, Team Twenty-Eight. Ninth, Westerley, second four.*"

There was a sound from somewhere behind Kiva, in the seventh row. It was not a word. It was the sound of a girl in sea blue, a Hold by her shoulders, who had been on the national roll a moment ago, and was not, and had not yet understood it. The boy beside her put his arm round her and turned her face into his coat.

Kiva did not look round. She looked at the back of Selka Dray's head, two rows in front.

Dray had not moved. She stood in Twenty-Eight's row with her weight on her right foot and her face, Kiva supposed, shut. Marten beside her had put his hand over his eyes. Dray did not put her hand anywhere. She stood, and the Graving's lamps lit the side of her face, and Kiva saw her jaw move once.

*The Hunt.* The board that had put Twenty-Eight on the roll by a tie was the one that had been read on Wednesday night under a line in a clerk's hand. *D. Penhale, catcher, the Saltmere Company, reserve to the officer, lost. The Board records it.* Dray had stood under that line and said *I'll take it. I'd not want anybody to think I was pleased.*

She was not pleased now. Kiva could see that from two rows back.

"*Seventh,*" said the Clerk. "*Team Seven, Meridian. Sixty-nine.*"

Kiva did not hear anything for a moment after that.

She was aware of Perrin at the other end of the row taking his hand off his side. She was aware of Anwen's shoulder against hers, quite hard, and of Jude, between them, very still, with the book under his good arm. She was aware of Pru behind her making a sound like somebody who has been holding their breath for a week. She was aware of the Graving making a sound as well, a long sound, from the Meridian knot in the middle tiers outward, that was partly the warm sound and partly something else.

*Sixty-nine.* Nineteen on the Bench, sixth, without her. Twenty-four on the Carry, first, on Perrin's back, up a ladder that he had climbed with a cracked rib on her word. Ten on the Hunt, fifteenth, with clean work on their stow and a pass in three that had held for a girl and been too late for a man. Sixteen on the Lane. Six to two with no fingers, four to five against Carrow's one door, six to five on the glass, with her on her knees beside the litter's head and Pru calling.

Not one mark of it was three doors. She had not opened three doors since the fifth of the tenth month, and she had not crossed once since Thursday the 10th. She had stood every lane of the Measure on one door. Seven was on the national roll for the year by one mark, and it had got there with a ladder, a stow, a hen, a pair of elbows and a girl who called the pen.

She found that this mattered more than she could have said. She had come two hundred miles with a thing in one hand that nobody else could do, and she had not used it. Seven had been put on the roll by a four and a method, by things with handles, that anybody in the Graving had watched and could do next year. Holloway's second four had done the hen to Halden this afternoon. The Ropewalk had done the elbows. Treen had called *far edge.*

She stood in the ninth row with her bound hand against her coat and did not cry, because it was not that kind of thing.

The Clerk went on reading. She read *sixth, Holloway, second four, seventy-six,* and *fifth, Holloway, first four, seventy-seven,* and *fourth, Old Four, Meridian, seventy-eight,* and Kiva heard Lowen Brask's flat voice say *thank you* to nobody from the second row, as a man says it to the ground. She read *third, Westerley, first four, seventy-nine.*

"*Second,*" said the Clerk. "*Halden License, Meridian. Eighty.*"

Kellan was in the front row. He did not move. Pettit beside him put his hand on Kellan's shoulder, quite heavily, as he had at the hurdle, and left it there.

"*First,*" said the Clerk of the Measure. "*The Ropewalk School, Saltmere, first four. Eighty-two.*"

The Graving stood up and shouted, and went on shouting.

Carrow did not move. She stood in the fourth row with Lyle on one side of her and Treen on the other and Tolver behind, with her edge dark on her right fist and her quick blunt face giving nothing away, and the whole town shouting for her from every tier. The nation's favourite, the Mercury had called her on the first day, a laid blade from a school with a column for two doors. She had won it with one door shut and a catcher's word.

She turned her head and looked along the rows until she found Seven. She found Kiva. She inclined her head, an inch, as a fact about the weather.

Kiva inclined hers.

"*The roll,*" said the Clerk, when the Graving would let her. "*The Ropewalk School, first four. Halden License. Westerley, first four. Old Four. Holloway, first four. Holloway, second four. Team Seven. Team Twenty-Eight.*" She did not read the marks again. "Licensed for the year. If the Board warrants a Barrel response and you go, you go by name, on the Board's warrant and the Board's ash." She paused. "The Board has been asked to warrant one for Wednesday morning, at the harbour pump house. It'll say tomorrow, at its sitting, at two, in this dock, after it's heard what it's asked to hear." She looked round the twenty-four rows. "No four on the roll is obliged to go. A license is not a warrant, and a warrant is not an order. I'll read it once more so that nobody can say they didn't hear it: no four is obliged to go."

She looked down the rows.

"The National Measure is read," she said. "The Board thanks the town of Saltmere for its dock. And for a great deal else." She did not say what else. "The Board records a man lost on the Longshore on Wednesday, in the course of the Measure, making the recovery the book prescribes. It'll go on recording him." She folded her hands. "That's all."

She walked off the chalk and went out by the side door.

---

Jude wrote it at the foot of the dock steps, under the first lamp, with the book on his arm and the crowd going up the steps round them.

He wrote the Lane first, because it was the day's. *Saturday the 19th. The Lane: Twenty-Eight, six to five. Pryce 0.15, Talley 0.11, Finch 0.12; Rowan her own thimble.* He did the sum in the margin, twice. *Lanes, three: thirty-one, forty-two, thirty-eight. A dram and eleven. Held, five and fifteen.*

"Five and fifteen," said Anwen.

"The most we've had at the end of anything." Jude did not look up. "And the tin, sealed. Two drams. For a hand. Not touched." He went on writing, under the ledger line, in his careful hand.

*The National Measure, at Saltmere. Bench, sixth. Carry, first. Hunt, fifteenth. Lane, ninth. Sixty-nine. Seventh of twenty-four. The national roll, by one mark.*

He looked at it for a long time. Then he scratched his jaw with his right hand.

"I'd want it said," he said, "that I don't know what it's worth yet. A license. I'll know on Wednesday." He shut the book on his finger. "I'll write it tonight anyway."

Perrin was sitting on the bottom step of the dock with his hand pressed to his side and his face grey. Aske was standing over him. Neither of them was saying anything. Perrin looked up at Kiva as she came, and his face did the thing it did when he was going to say something he had been holding.

"One mark," he said. "One. Westerley's second four were on the roll till she read the tie. I watched the girl in sea blue."

"So did I."

"I'd thought it would feel like the top of the ladder." He breathed carefully. "It feels like that girl's face." He was quiet a moment. "I'll take it. I'd not give it back. I'm only saying."

"I know," said Kiva.

"And we won the Carry," said Perrin. "Outright. One board out of four, outright, on the national. That's ours." He let Aske take his wrist. "That's the ladder. That's the one with my name on it."

"It's got all our names on it," said Anwen.

"I know. That's why I like it."

---

The transit came into the high town's station at twenty-five to ten, and Mara was on the platform, and Kiva was beside her, because the page was hers.

It was a long low iron shed at the top of the town, lit white. The transit came in under it with a long sigh of brakes, three carriages, and stopped, and the doors opened, and people got down who had come two hundred miles in a day and a half and looked it. Kiva watched them go past. A man in Works grey. Two women with a basket. A clerk with a satchel.

Then a small woman got down from the second carriage with two pairs of spectacles, one on her nose and one pushed up into her grey hair, and a leather satchel held in both arms against her chest as though it were a baby. Behind her a porter carried a grey box tied with tape, with a number on its end and a red card in its holder, and behind him a second porter carried a black calf book under one arm, with a straw in it.

The Reader of the Compact Library looked up and down the platform with her bright eyes, and found Mara, and came straight to her.

"Mrs. Rowan," she said. "Miss Rowan." She did not put the satchel down. "I've not let go of this since eight o'clock yesterday morning. I slept with it on my lap. The guard asked whether it was a cat." She looked at Mara over the lower spectacles. "It's sealed as it went in. My wax over yours. The hour through the join. I've looked at it every hour since the Library and it's not moved." She opened the satchel, and took out a single sheet folded in three, with brown wax across the fold and black wax over the brown, and figures written across the join so that half of each lay on either side. *Eight minutes to two. Monday the 25th. Received shut. L. Arkwright.*

She held it out.

"To your hand," she said. "As I said. Not your niece's. Yours."

Mara took it. Her reading hand closed on it, and Kiva saw the hand not shake, and saw what it cost.

"And the box," said the Reader. "Annick, Personal. Under my custody. The settlement says it may be produced to a magistrate. I've decided that a Board sitting in a dry dock with half a town watching is near enough a magistrate for a reading room." She almost smiled. "If the Order's Master objects, I'll take it back to Meridian unread, and say so, and nobody will think the worse of either of us."

"She'll not object," said Mara. "I don't think."

"Nor do I. I've read her letters to the Library about those boxes for twenty years." The Reader nodded at the second porter. "And Mr. Lumb's book, with his compliments, which were not very civilly put. He says it's never been out of the pump house since 2604 and he'd like it back before the eleventh month." She looked at Kiva at last, properly, with her bright eyes. "You read it in the room. In the summer. Your lips counted."

"Yes."

"I wrote that down. I write everything down." The Reader shut the satchel. "Tomorrow they'll read it aloud in a dock. All of it. Yours and hers and the keepers'." She looked at Kiva for a long moment. "I've kept that page in the dark for nine months so that it could be believed. I'd like you to know I've never once been curious what's on it. I'll be curious tomorrow."

---

Anwen was waiting at the foot of Quiller's stair at a quarter past ten, with her hands in her sleeves.

She had not gone up. The loft's window was lit above, and Kiva could hear Old Four by the stove arguing about the Lane. Anwen stood on the bottom step in the dark of Quay Street with the gratings rattling in the gutter on the count, and when Kiva came along from the Long Quay she did not move off the step.

"Pryce," said Kiva.

"I've a card," said Anwen. "Rhea Sorn pinned it at the baize at noon, after our lane. I read it at one. I took it down at two." She took one hand out of her sleeve with the card in it, slate blue, and held it out. "Read it. I want you first. Before Perrin, before my mother. I've decided, and I want it said plainly, and I want you to hear it before anybody tells you."

Kiva took it in her left hand and read it by the light from Quiller's window.

*Graywater. Pryce, A., Hold, Meridian, Team Seven. To Doran Mace's ward line on the night van, in her own place and not a relief's, from the first of the fourth month, at a ward-hand's wage. Mace asked for her by name in the autumn and left her name where it was. A card is not a seat. R. Sorn.*

On the back, in Anwen's hand, square and pressed hard into the card: *Yes. A. Pryce.*

"It's not a relief," said Anwen. "It's a place. Mine. Mace's line. At home." She did not go red. Anwen never went red. "I'll be on the van from the first of next month, two nights a week, and the Hall the rest. I'll not be in Seven's lanes after the summer. Not every one." She looked at Kiva in the dark. "I'm not leaving. I'm going somewhere. I wanted you to know the difference, from me."

Kiva stood on the cobbles with the slate-blue card in her hand.

She thought of a stair in the Hall a year ago, and a girl who would not look at her. She thought of a wax seal and a sealed tin, and a wall walked from its far end round a planted girl in the Graving that morning, and Lyle on her knees nearly smiling. She thought of Anwen saying in a shut shop *I'll be at your back with my wall up.*

"You taught Lyle to lean," she said. "On Thursday. And she leaned on us on Friday."

"I know she did."

"Mace told Rhea you'd give it away to anybody who asked properly."

"Mace says that about everybody he likes." Anwen took the card back, carefully, and put it inside her coat. "I'd give it away again. That's what it's for. A wall nobody else knows how to walk is only a wall." She was quiet a moment. "You'd know. You've been saying that about yourself all winter."

"I'm glad," said Kiva. "And I'll miss you in the lanes. Both."

Anwen looked at her for a long moment. Then she did a thing she had not done since a kitchen table in Coper Street in the autumn. She put her arms round Kiva, quickly and hard, on the bottom step of Quiller's stair, with the gratings rattling in the gutter, and let go before either of them had to say anything about it.

"Go up," she said. "You've the Board in the morning, and a man with his hand on a glass case."

"Nine o'clock."

"Nine o'clock." Anwen went up the stair ahead of her. At the top she stopped with her hand on the door. "Kiva. Whatever's on that page. I'll be there at two."

"I know you will," said Kiva.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-39.md

# Chapter 39 — Kiva: The Last Witness

Orla Dane knocked on the Chair's door at the officers' lodging at six on Sunday morning with the head of her stick, because her knee would not let her stand long enough to wait for an answer to her knuckles.

Ysra Crest opened it herself. She was dressed. Orla had known she would be. She had a pen in her hand and a sheet of paper under it, and behind her on the lodging's narrow table there was a lamp turned low and the Chair's ledger lying open beside the thick cream letter that had come to her from the Long Quay a week ago in a large, upright, old hand.

"Dane," said the Chair.

"Crest." Orla did not come in. "Two minutes. Then I'll go down to the pump house and you'll go to a dining room on the quay with a man who can't stand up. I'd sooner say it before than after."

The Chair stood aside. Orla came in, stick first, and sat down on the room's one hard chair without being asked, because she would have fallen over otherwise. The Chair did not sit. She stood by the table with the pen in her hand.

"You've answered him," said Orla, looking at the cream letter.

"An hour ago." The Chair turned the sheet under her pen round on the table so that Orla could read it. It was very short, in the level hand that Orla had read on every condition Kiva Rowan had ever been given.

*To Mr. A. Halden, the Counting House. You asked me on the 12th, in your own hand, to ask Miss Rowan for the sequence of three doors, on any terms I set. I set none. The sequence is not to be asked of her. That stands as I wrote it in the spring. If the country wishes to unwrite it, the country may ask me in a room in front of everybody, and I shall say no in the same room. I thank you for asking properly. Y. Crest, Placement Chair, Meridian.*

Orla read it twice, with her lips shut.

"She said that at his table," she said. "In nearly those words."

"I know. Adlam told me what she said, in the street, the morning after." The Chair took the sheet back. "I'd like it said twice, in two hands. Once by the girl, at a dinner. Once by the Chair, on paper. Then nobody can say the first was a child being rude to an old man, or the second a clerk hiding behind a rule." She blotted it. "What are your two minutes?"

Orla looked at her stick for a moment.

"My line," she said. "On her certificate. The sponsor's. *Recovery only, dark hands; and one crossing.* My standing rides on her record. Every condition you've written since the spring has my name somewhere under it." She did not look up. "I'd like you to strike it."

The Chair did not move.

"Not today," said Orla. "When it's no longer needed. I want it asked now, in this room, and written down, so it's been asked before and not after. I've held things back in case I was wrong. A card for twenty years. I'll not hold this one."

"When is no longer needed?"

"When she makes a crossing in the field, inside her certificate, on her own word, with nobody's standing riding on it but hers." Orla's crooked face did not change. "I'll know when. So will you. It may be Wednesday. It may be never. If it's never, leave my name where it is and I'll be glad of it."

The Chair looked at her for a long time across the narrow room, with the lamp between them. They had stood on opposite sides of a Bench in 2660, Orla at the rail with her candidate and Crest on the ward, and they had both watched a boy's hand go, and they had never once spoken of it in twenty years.

"Why?" said the Chair.

"Because she stood up in a hall on Friday and put a thing on a table under her own name that she'd been keeping since she was thirteen." Orla's voice was rough and brief. "And on Saturday she knelt on a floor in front of the whole town on one door, because she'd decided what her crossing was for, and nobody had told her. My line says she's mine to answer for. She's not. She's hers." She put both hands on her stick. "A line that says otherwise when it isn't true is a lie in your ledger. I'd not have that, at my age."

The Chair turned to the ledger and took up her pen and wrote, standing, at the foot of the page that had Kiva Rowan's conditions on it. Orla watched the pen move. When it stopped, the Chair turned the ledger round.

*Requested by the accountable sponsor, O. Dane, Sunday the 20th of the third month, 2681, at six in the morning: that the sponsor's line be struck when the certificate is able to stand in the candidate's own name. To be decided by the Chair on evidence. Y. C.*

"On evidence," said Orla.

"Always." The Chair capped her pen. "You'll be at the Graving at two."

"On a bench at the back. Where I can get out."

"Then I'll see you there." The Chair hesitated, which Orla had never seen her do. "He'll be at the Graving as well. Hart. He's said he'll stand and say what the hand says, if he's asked again."

"I know he will," said Orla. "I'm going to the pump house now. Before. Not after."

---

He was on the gallery at a quarter to seven, oiling the second pump, with his book of slacks in his jacket and his bad hand at his side.

It was the middle of a neap ebb, and the basin was going out quietly under the sea gate, brown and slow. The six pumps went up and down on their stroke, and the thirty-six cells in their tall glass cases turned their lights together, four and four, and the whole hall hummed with it like a held note. Orla came up the iron stair a step at a time with her stick ringing on each tread, and he heard her at the bottom, and did not turn round until she was at the top.

"Dane," he said.

"Jess."

Nobody had called him that in this building, she thought, but Peverell, and Peverell said it like the name of a tool. Orla had not said it since 2660. It came out of her mouth rough and strange, like a word in a language she had stopped speaking.

He put down the oil can.

They stood on the gallery with the cells between them. She had come here a fortnight ago, on a Sunday, and said the wrong thing first and not found the right one after. He had told her so. She had walked down this stair afterward and told the girl it had gone badly, and the girl had told her that Hart had said the same thing to her. *It went badly on both sides.* She had not known whether to be glad of that. She was not, she found, glad of it now. She only wanted to say a true thing to him and go down the stair.

"I'll say one thing," she said. "I've had it ready since the boat. I'll say it badly. It's true."

He waited. He had always been good at waiting. She had taught him that, on a floor, when he was fifteen.

"I didn't write after the Bench," said Orla, "because I was afraid you'd write back and tell me it was my fault, and it was."

The pumps went up and down. One of the cells in the north gallery flickered, as a cell does, and steadied.

Hart did not say anything for a long time.

"It was mine as well," he said at last. "I went onto that Bench with a door I knew wasn't ready, because I wanted you to see me do it. I'd have gone if you'd forbidden me." He looked at his bad hand, at his side, with the fingers half curled. "I've known that for twenty years. I've not said it to anybody."

"That doesn't make it not mine."

"No. It makes it both." He picked up the oil can again. "I'll not say it's all right, Dane. It's not. I'd have liked a letter."

"I'd not believe you if you said it was."

"I know you wouldn't. That's why I'm not." He bent to the pump's crank and put a drop of oil on it, very carefully, with his left hand. "Go down. You've a girl standing up in a dock at two, and I've a hand to stand up beside her. I'll see you there."

She went down. At the foot of the stair she stopped and looked back up at the gallery, where he was oiling the pump with his back to her, and she thought that nothing had been mended. Nothing was going to be mended. It had been said, by both of them, in a building full of things keeping time, and it would stay said.

That was not nothing. She had thought, for twenty years, that it would be.

---

The Counting House was lit at nine on Sunday morning, every window, as it had been lit at eight on a Friday night a week ago, white and steady all the way up.

Kiva came along the Long Quay with Mara beside her and the Chair a pace behind. The Clerk of the Measure was already on the step, small and grey, with her gold chain on her coat and the two Board members flanking her: the heavy man with his spectacles pushed up on his forehead, and the thin woman with ink on her cuff and a ledger under her arm. Halden's counsel stood a little apart in his neat grey coat with his folder against his chest. Nobody else had been let come. The Clerk had said so in the Salt Hall, and she had said it again on the step, to the Mercury's young man, who had followed them along the quay with three pencils and stood now at the bottom of the step looking as though he might cry.

"Two o'clock," the Clerk told him. "In the Graving. You'll hear all of it read."

"It's not the same as hearing it said, ma'am."

"No," said the Clerk. "It's not. That's why the room's not large."

A man in Halden's livery opened the door before anybody knocked. Inside, there were the lamps: on brass brackets, in glass globes on the newel posts, in the great iron ring slung from the stairwell's ceiling, every one of them white and not one of them flickering. Kiva did not count them this time. She had made up her mind on the quay not to.

On the fourth step, her heart began to slow.

She had known it would. She felt it come down, and come down again on the tenth step, and by the top of the stair it was going slowly and evenly and heavily, like a pump. She put her left hand in her coat pocket and kept it there. She did not lay her fingers on the banister. She knew what the banister would say.

Mara, on the stair beside her, had her left hand in her pocket too.

"You've gone white," said Mara, very low.

"I know."

"So have I." Mara did not look at her. "Fifty-seven, I'd say. I've never been fifty-seven in my life."

The long room was as it had been. The windows at both ends, one over the harbour and one over the high town. The fire. The long dark table, polished, with nothing on it but a white cloth at the far end and two candlesticks and a glass case with a brass frame. Mr. Adlam stood by the harbour window with his hat in his hand and his long face astonished, which it was not.

And at the head of the table, with his left hand flat on the case, sat the Proprietor.

He did not get up. He looked at them as they came in, one after another, with his lean face and his dark hair grey at the temples, a man of fifty-five by his face and a hundred and three by every other measure. He looked at the Clerk and the Board, and the Chair, and his own counsel, and Mara, and Kiva last.

"Forgive me for sitting," he said. His voice was pleasant and dry, as it had been. "I can't rise without lifting my hand, and I don't lift it. Miss Rowan knows."

"Yes," said Kiva.

"Then she'll know where to sit." He did not smile. "Miss Rowan. I asked you to sit further off a week ago, and you didn't, and I was sorry. I'll not ask today. You'll sit where you choose."

Kiva went to the far end of the table. All the way. She sat down in the last chair, thirty feet from him, with the harbour window behind her and the white cloth and the two candlesticks between them, and felt her heart come up a beat, two beats, as though somebody had let go of a rope. Her Ember settled under her breastbone. Her still door, which had wanted to beat on the stair, lay still.

"Thank you," said the Proprietor. "That's the right chair. I was too proud to say so last time."

Mara sat beside her. The Chair sat on the window side. The Clerk sat at the middle of the table, halfway between, with the two Board members on either side, and the thin woman opened her ledger and dipped her pen. Halden's counsel stood behind his master's chair and did not sit.

"The Charter Board," said the Clerk, "takes the evidence of Mr. Ambrose Halden, at his request, under his own name, at his own table, because he can't come to the Board. What's said will be written down by the Board's clerk and read in the Graving at two, in full." She folded her hands on the table. "Mr. Halden."

He told it to the harbour window.

He told it as he had told it to Kiva a week ago, at the same table, in nearly the same words, and she found that she could hear the places where the words had worn smooth from being said to himself for seventy-seven years. A bench clerk on the second floor, twenty-six, four years in the house, who had written nothing in his life but counts. *Fell in at fifty to the minute.* A fever on the evening of the 11th of the third month, 2604. A window at three in the morning. Every lamp on the Long Quay going white, very bright, and staying lit; every lamp in the low town going out, all at once, as if a hand had been put over them; and half an hour; and the low town's lamps coming on again of themselves; and no fault found.

The thin woman wrote it down.

"That's the window," said the Proprietor. "I've said it at dinners. Mr. Renn's heard it four times. I've said it to the Board's officers before, and to the Works, and to every keeper in the country who'd let me read his book for that night." He was quiet for a moment. "I've never said the rest. To anybody. In seventy-seven years."

Nobody moved.

"I dreamed that night," said the Proprietor. "With the fever. Before the window. I was in my bed on the third floor of this house, and I dreamed there was no roof on it, nor on anything. Nothing over the town, and nothing over me. It was not dark. It was not empty." His voice did not change at all. "There was something in it. Paying attention. I've no better words than that, and I've had seventy-seven years to look for some."

The heavy Board member had taken his spectacles off his forehead and was holding them in his hand.

"I woke," said the Proprietor, "and went to the window, and the lamps went white." He turned his head from the harbour window and looked down the length of the table at Kiva. "I've dreamed it eleven times since. Not often. Not on any night I could choose. Always with a fever, and always near a thing I can't name, and never once since 2611 when I didn't have my hand on this glass. The last was in the ninth month of last year. The 17th. In the evening."

*The 17th of the ninth month. Twenty past two.* The bank in this harbour, falling into step. The Pit. The lamp-man at Holloway with a fever two nights, who would not have it written.

"I've told nobody," said the Proprietor. "Not my house. Not my counsel." He did not look round at the neat grey man standing behind his chair. "I'm a hundred and three years old, and I hold the foundation's pattern, and the foundation's patent, and the country's largest yard, and I was ashamed. A Proprietor with a fever who dreams the sky's gone. They'd have taken the glass off me in a week, and they'd have been right to." He looked down at his hand on the case. "And then on Friday night a girl of seventeen with two dead fingers stood up in the Salt Hall in front of the whole town, under her own name, and said she'd had it. With a sealed page to prove she'd not borrowed it." He looked back up at Kiva. "Mr. Adlam came along the quay to tell me at a quarter to nine. I wrote to the Board at ten to. I'd have been ashamed not to."

He turned to the thin woman with the ledger.

"Write that I've dreamed it," he said. "Ambrose Halden. The Proprietor. Under my own name. In 2604, and eleven times since."

She wrote it.

"There's a thing I've thought since 2604," said the Proprietor, "and I'll say it now, because it's why I've done what I've done with my life, and the Board should know why a man's done what he's done before it decides he was wrong."

He did not wait for the Clerk to let him.

"After that night," he said, "I went looking for anybody else who'd seen it. Every fault book in the country. Every keeper. I found the Ferren keeper's line, which says the levee lamps went out, and nothing about which. And I found that the Order of the Even Hand had a Master, once, who'd written a fever down in a notebook, and that a Halden heir had taken her papers out of the Order's house the year I came to this one, and given them to the Library to make himself look generous." His mouth moved. "I went to the Library in 2610 and asked to read it. The settlement over that box was made in 2611, and I was the reason; the Order heard that a Halden had read their Master's private notebook, and went to law. I've never regretted reading it. I've regretted that a great many times."

"What did it say?" said the Clerk.

"It's in the box that came on last night's transit, I'm told. It'll be read at two." The Proprietor looked at his hand on the glass. "But I'll say the one line, because I've lived by it, and the Board should hear it from me as well as from the page. *Every lamp in Meridian answers at once. Some go out. Some go white.*"

Kiva sat very still at the far end of the table.

"Some go white," said the Proprietor. "Some go out. And on the Long Quay in 2604, the lamps that went white were the lamps that kept time together, and the lamps that went out were the lamps that kept their own." He did not raise his voice. "That's my reading. I've had it since I was twenty-seven. If whatever comes, comes again, I want this country's lamps white and not out. I want every bank stepped, every pump house, every box in every tenement, all keeping one time, so that whatever comes finds them lit." He looked at the Clerk. "That's why the foundation claims what it claims. Not for the ash. I don't want the ash. I want the country ready."

"And you're certain," said the heavy Board member.

"I'm sure of my reading," said the Proprietor. "I've been sure of it for seventy-seven years. I'm not certain I'm right." He said it quite simply, as he might have said the time. "Those are two different things, sir. I've been a clerk. I know the difference between a count and what it means." He turned his head and looked down the table at Kiva again. "Which is why I asked the Board to bring Miss Rowan. I'd like to ask her one question. Through the Board, if the Board allows it. She's not obliged to answer."

The Clerk looked at Kiva.

"You're not obliged," she said.

"I know," said Kiva.

"Then ask it, Mr. Halden."

The Proprietor looked at her for a long moment, down thirty feet of polished wood, past the two candlesticks and the white cloth. Her heart was going steadily. Her still door was still. Her hands were hot in her lap.

"The ferry," he said. "Below the Order's house. Last month. The Mercury says you were lower than before, and that the lights were nearer, and that some were going out." He did not lean forward; he could not. "Were any of them white?"

She had known he would ask. She had known since the shop on Thursday night, when she held the ferry back from her four and told them she did not yet know whom it was for. She had known on a gallery in a pump house, and on the quay with Kellan, who had said *every time he gets to white his voice goes quiet.* She had known all night, lying awake by the north window with the Mercury under her pillow.

It was the one thing she had seen that would please him.

If she said *yes,* every word of it would go into the thin woman's ledger, under the Proprietor's own line, and be read at two in the Graving to the whole town, and printed in the Mercury in the morning beside *some go white* in a Glory master's hand. Halden's counsel would have it in his folder by tonight. It would be a stone in the foundation's wall. It would be read by every man in the country who wanted his lamps white and not out, and it would mean, to every one of them, that the Proprietor had been right.

She did not know that it meant that. She did not know what it meant at all.

If she said *no,* or said nothing, he would never know. Nobody would. It was hers alone. She had told Mara everything for four years at a kitchen table, and she had not told Mara this. It was the one piece of the dream she had kept, even from the page.

She thought of a lamp-man in Holloway who would not have it written, because he would be laughed at. She thought that he had not been the only one keeping a thing back for fear of what it would be taken to mean. The Proprietor had kept his for seventy-seven years, from shame. She had been about to keep hers for the opposite reason. She would be keeping it because she did not like whose side it would be put on.

That was not a reason. That was choosing a side by leaving a thing out of a column, and she had watched her aunt, in the autumn, refuse to hide a line on a mast that she did not understand.

"Yes," said Kiva.

The room was very quiet.

"Some had gone white," she said. "On the ferry. Not on Classification Day, and not when I was thirteen. Only the last time. I was lower than I'd been, and the lights on the roads were nearer, and I could see them better. Some were bright and some were dull. Some were going out. And some had gone white."
